```text
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/related_work.tex:44:Our neural probe experiment follows this protocol: a two-layer MLP on frozen Qwen2.5-1.5B embeddings, where the probe's limited capacity ensures that discriminability reflects depth information already present in the representations rather than learned de novo.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/conclusion.tex:45:A preliminary neural probing experiment suggests that this operational bound is not tight: a frozen Qwen2.5-1.5B encoder paired with an MLP classifier achieves $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}} = 3$ on the Qwen-continuation dataset (2v3 $\auc = 0.6195$ versus 0.5609 with hand-crafted features), indicating that learned representations recover depth signal beyond the 15-dimensional feature set.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/conclusion.tex:57:[Omitted long matching line]
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/experiments_c1.tex:344:\subsection{Representation Dependence of $\Kempirical$}
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/experiments_c1.tex:345:\label{sec:neural_probe}
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/experiments_c1.tex:352:We extract last-layer hidden states from a frozen Qwen2.5-1.5B model (\texttt{float16}), apply attention-masked average pooling to obtain 1536-dimensional document embeddings, and train a two-hidden-layer MLP ($1536 \to 512 \to 256 \to 6$, ReLU, dropout 0.1) with 5-fold GroupKFold cross-validation on the Qwen-continuation dataset (Table~\ref{tab:s7_neural_probe}).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/experiments_c1.tex:354:% TODO(exp-015-rerun): Pythia/OLMo neural probe setup details here
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/experiments_c1.tex:357:% TODO(exp-015-rerun): cross-model neural probe comparison table
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/experiments_c1.tex:359:The neural probe achieves 6-class accuracy of 0.4335 (vs.\ 0.3875 for LightGBM with 15D features).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/experiments_c1.tex:373:The neural probe's 2v3 $\auc = 0.6195$ ($\text{std} = 0.0094$ across folds) exceeds $\theta = 0.60$, yielding $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}} = 3$---one discriminable boundary beyond what the 15D feature set recovers on the same data.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/experiments_c1.tex:375:The improvement concentrates at the 1v2 and 2v3 boundaries ($+$8.7 and $+$5.9\,pp, respectively), where the 15D features lose most of their discriminative power; at the already-trivial 0v1 boundary the neural probe is marginally \emph{lower} ($-$1.0\,pp), consistent with both representations saturating the human--synthetic gap.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/experiments_c1.tex:376:At 3v4, the neural probe reaches $\auc = 0.5930$---below $\theta$ but closer to the threshold than any 15D configuration---hinting that a still richer representation might push the boundary further.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/experiments_c1.tex:393:Notably, the neural probe's advantage does not alter the headline result for practitioners: $\Kempirical = 2$ remains the relevant boundary for deployable depth estimation with current feature technology.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/experiments_c1.tex:399:% TODO(exp-015-rerun): bootstrap CIs for neural probe pairwise AUC
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/experiments_c1.tex:400:% TODO(exp-015-rerun): cross-model neural probe comparison table
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/experiments_c1.tex:401:Critically, the current neural probe data covers only the Qwen-continuation dataset; cross-model validation on Pythia and OLMo is pending.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/discussion.tex:30:A frozen Qwen2.5-1.5B encoder (1536-dimensional last-layer representations) paired with a lightweight MLP classifier (Table~\ref{tab:s7_neural_probe}) achieves 2v3 $\auc = 0.6195$ ($\text{std} = 0.0094$) on the Qwen-continuation dataset, exceeding the $\theta = 0.60$ threshold that the same dataset's LightGBM classifier fails to cross ($\auc = 0.5609$; Section~\ref{sec:kstar_boundary}).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/discussion.tex:31:This yields $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}} = 3$: the learned representation recovers one additional discriminable boundary that the hand-crafted features miss.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/discussion.tex:32:The improvement is concentrated at the 2v3 pair ($+$5.86\,pp over LightGBM); at the already-separable 1v2 boundary the neural probe achieves 0.6983 versus 0.6112, a substantial gain but one that does not alter the boundary determination.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/discussion.tex:33:At 3v4, the neural probe reaches $\auc = 0.5930$---below $\theta$ but closer to the threshold than any hand-crafted configuration---suggesting that a still richer representation might push the boundary further.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/discussion.tex:137:This narrow margin means that small changes in the feature set or classifier could shift the boundary---as the neural probe results confirm, where a richer representation pushes the 2v3 pair above $\theta$ on the Qwen-continuation dataset (Section~\ref{sec:discuss_kstar}).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/discussion.tex:145:However, the neural probe result rests on a single dataset and awaits cross-model validation and bootstrap confidence intervals before a firm revision of the headline $\Kempirical$ can be claimed.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/discussion.tex:149:A preliminary neural probing experiment demonstrates that frozen LLM embeddings (1536 dimensions) paired with a lightweight MLP push $\Kempirical$ from~2 to~3 on the Qwen-continuation dataset (Section~\ref{sec:discuss_kstar}), confirming that learned representations capture depth-discriminative patterns that statistical features miss.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/method.tex:87:We use hand-crafted statistical features rather than learned representations for three reasons: (1) they are directly interpretable, enabling degradation ordering analysis across model families; (2) they transfer across generators without retraining the feature extractor; and (3) they permit attribution of discriminative power to specific text properties, which is essential for understanding \emph{how} the depth signal degrades (full feature definitions in Table~\ref{tab:s5_features}).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/appendix.tex:197:%%% Table S7: Neural Probe Architecture (exp-015)
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/appendix.tex:200:  \caption{Neural probe architecture and training details (Exp-015). Frozen Qwen2.5-1.5B hidden states are average-pooled into 1536-dimensional document embeddings, then classified by a two-hidden-layer MLP. This probe validates that the $\Kempirical = 2$ boundary found with hand-crafted features is data-intrinsic rather than a feature engineering artifact.}
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/appendix.tex:201:  \label{tab:s7_neural_probe}
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/introduction.tex:22:The choice of interpretable statistical features---rather than learned representations---is deliberate: it enables cross-generator transfer without retraining and permits attribution of discriminative power to specific text properties.
```

```text
342	
343	%% ====================================================================
344	\subsection{Representation Dependence of $\Kempirical$}
345	\label{sec:neural_probe}
346	
347	The preceding sections establish $\Kempirical = 2$ using a 15-dimensional hand-crafted feature set.
348	A natural question is whether this boundary reflects a fundamental limit of the generational depth signal or an information bottleneck imposed by projecting high-dimensional text representations into 15 statistical summaries.
349	To disentangle these possibilities, we replace the hand-crafted features with frozen LLM hidden states and train a lightweight MLP probe on the same classification task.
350	
351	\paragraph{Setup.}
352	We extract last-layer hidden states from a frozen Qwen2.5-1.5B model (\texttt{float16}), apply attention-masked average pooling to obtain 1536-dimensional document embeddings, and train a two-hidden-layer MLP ($1536 \to 512 \to 256 \to 6$, ReLU, dropout 0.1) with 5-fold GroupKFold cross-validation on the Qwen-continuation dataset (Table~\ref{tab:s7_neural_probe}).
353	The probe's limited capacity ensures that any discriminability reflects depth information already encoded in the frozen representations rather than patterns learned de novo.
354	% TODO(exp-015-rerun): Pythia/OLMo neural probe setup details here
355	
356	\paragraph{Results.}
357	% TODO(exp-015-rerun): cross-model neural probe comparison table
358	Preliminary results on the Qwen-continuation dataset are reported below; cross-model validation on Pythia and OLMo is pending.
359	The neural probe achieves 6-class accuracy of 0.4335 (vs.\ 0.3875 for LightGBM with 15D features).
360	The pairwise $\auc$ profile reveals a shifted discrimination boundary:
361	\begin{center}
362	\small
363	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
364	\toprule
365	 & 0v1 & 1v2 & 2v3 & 3v4 & 4v5 \\
366	\midrule
367	15D (LightGBM)   & 0.997 & 0.611 & 0.561 & 0.541 & 0.514 \\
368	Neural (MLP)      & 0.987 & 0.698 & 0.620 & 0.593 & 0.575 \\
369	$\Delta$\,(pp)    & $-$1.0 & $+$8.7 & $+$5.9 & $+$5.2 & $+$6.1 \\
370	\bottomrule
371	\end{tabular}
372	\end{center}
373	The neural probe's 2v3 $\auc = 0.6195$ ($\text{std} = 0.0094$ across folds) exceeds $\theta = 0.60$, yielding $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}} = 3$---one discriminable boundary beyond what the 15D feature set recovers on the same data.
374	The 15D classifier on the same dataset gives 2v3 $\auc = 0.5609$ (Section~\ref{sec:kstar_boundary}), well below $\theta$.
375	The improvement concentrates at the 1v2 and 2v3 boundaries ($+$8.7 and $+$5.9\,pp, respectively), where the 15D features lose most of their discriminative power; at the already-trivial 0v1 boundary the neural probe is marginally \emph{lower} ($-$1.0\,pp), consistent with both representations saturating the human--synthetic gap.
376	At 3v4, the neural probe reaches $\auc = 0.5930$---below $\theta$ but closer to the threshold than any 15D configuration---hinting that a still richer representation might push the boundary further.
377	
378	\paragraph{Interpretation: $\Kstar$ hierarchy.}
379	These preliminary results motivate a three-level hierarchy of discrimination boundaries:
380	\[
381	  \Ktheory \;\geq\; \Kempirical^{\text{neural}} \;\geq\; \Kempirical^{\text{15D}}
382	\]
383	where $\Kempirical^{\text{15D}} = 2$ is the operationally deployable boundary measured with model-free, interpretable features, and $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}} = 3$ is a representation-dependent ceiling accessible only through model-specific probing.
384	The gap between the two confirms that the 15D feature set does not saturate the available depth signal: at least one additional discriminable generation boundary exists in the frozen representations but is lost during projection into the hand-crafted feature space.
385	The theoretical upper bound $\Ktheory$---the boundary achievable with an optimal representation and classifier---remains unknown but is at least~3.
386	
387	\paragraph{Interpretability--discriminability tradeoff.}
388	The two $\Kempirical$ estimates serve complementary purposes.
389	The 15D boundary ($\Kempirical = 2$) is model-free, interpretable, and computationally lightweight: features are extracted from surface-level token statistics without access to model internals, and the LightGBM classifier trains in seconds.
390	This makes it suitable for deployment in data curation pipelines.
391	The neural boundary ($\Kempirical^{\text{neural}} = 3$) requires a frozen copy of a specific LLM for embedding extraction, is opaque in the features it exploits, and incurs $100\times$+ computational overhead for inference.
392	It is valuable as a theoretical exploration tool---demonstrating that deeper depth signals \emph{exist}---but impractical for the model-agnostic deployment scenarios where depth estimation is most needed.
393	Notably, the neural probe's advantage does not alter the headline result for practitioners: $\Kempirical = 2$ remains the relevant boundary for deployable depth estimation with current feature technology.
394	
395	\paragraph{Caveats.}
396	The 2v3 boundary under neural probing is marginal: the mean $\auc$ of 0.6195 exceeds $\theta = 0.60$ by only $+$1.95\,pp, and individual fold variation (std $= 0.0094$) places this margin within approximately two standard deviations of the threshold.
397	These results should be treated as preliminary evidence that the 15D boundary is representation-dependent, not as a firm revision of $\Kempirical$.
398	% TODO(exp-015-rerun): Pythia/OLMo K*(neural) results here
399	% TODO(exp-015-rerun): bootstrap CIs for neural probe pairwise AUC
400	% TODO(exp-015-rerun): cross-model neural probe comparison table
401	Critically, the current neural probe data covers only the Qwen-continuation dataset; cross-model validation on Pythia and OLMo is pending.
402	Until confirmed across model families, $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}} = 3$ should be interpreted as a single-dataset preliminary finding rather than a general result.
403	
```

argparse\\nimport json\\nimport os\\nfrom abc import ABC, abstractmethod\\nfrom collections import Counter\\nfrom hashlib import md5\\n\\nimport numpy as np\\nimport pandas as pd\\n\\nFEATURE_COLS = [\\n    \\\"surp_mean\\\", \\\"surp_std\\\", \\\"surp_skew\\\", \\\"surp_kurt\\\",\\n    \\\"surp_d1_mean\\\", \\\"surp_d1_std\\\", \\\"surp_d1_skew\\\",\\n    \\\"surp_d2_mean\\\", \\\"surp_d2_std\\\",\\n    \\\"ttr\\\", \\\"hapax_ratio\\\", \\\"self_bleu\\\",\\n    \\\"freq_kurtosis\\\", \\\"freq_entropy\\\", \\\"low_freq_ratio\\\",\\n]\\n\\n\\nclass FilterStrategy(ABC):\\n    @abstractmethod\\n    def fit(self, features: np.ndarray, depths: np.ndarray, texts: list = None):\\n        ...\\n\\n    @abstractmethod\\n    def get_weights(self, features: np.ndarray, texts: list = None) -> np.ndarray:\\n        ...\\n\\n    def get_mask(self, features: np.ndarray, texts: list = None, threshold: float = 0.0) -> np.ndarray:\\n        return self.get_weights(features, texts) > threshold\\n\\n\\nclass NoFilter(FilterStrategy):\\n    def fit(self, features, depths, texts=None):\\n        pass\\n\\n    def get_weights(self, features, texts=None):\\n        return np.ones(len(features))\\n\\n\\nclass BinaryOurs(FilterStrategy):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"LightGBM binary: human (depth=0) vs synthetic (depth>=1).\\n    Weight = 1 - P(synthetic). Hard mask at P(synthetic) < threshold.\\n    Uses same 15D features as Graduated for fair comparison (C3 claim).\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n\\n    def __init__(self, threshold=0.5):\\n        self.threshold = threshold\\n        self.model = None\\n\\n    def fit(self, features, depths, texts=None):\\n        import lightgbm as lgb\\n        labels = (depths > 0).astype(int)\\n        self.model = lgb.LGBMClassifier(\\n            n_estimators=200, max_depth=6, learning_rate=0.05,\\n            subsample=0.8, colsample_bytree=0.8,\\n            random_state=42, verbose=-1,\\n        )\\n        self.model.fit(features, labels)\\n\\n    def get_weights(self, features, texts=None):\\n        return 1.0 - self.model.predict_proba(features)[:, 1]\\n\\n    def get_mask(self, features, texts=None, threshold=None):\\n        t = threshold if threshold is not None else self.threshold\\n        proba_syn = self.model.predict_proba(features)[:, 1]\\n        return proba_syn < t\\n\\n\\nclass BinaryDrayson(FilterStrategy):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"SIR (Drayson et al. 2025): w_j = (1-q(x_j))^b / Z, resample k*n.\\n\\n    SIR parameters from Drayson et al. (2025), Table S1:\\n    b=1, k=1.5, rmax=3 (for SmolLM-1.7B, closest to our Qwen-1.5B).\\n    Values selected by evaluating on Wikitext-2 validation set after 1 generation.\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n\\n    def __init__(self, b=1, k=1.5, rmax=3, seed=42):\\n        self.b = b\\n        self.k = k\\n        self.rmax = rmax\\n        self.seed = seed\\n        self.model = None\\n\\n    def fit(self, features, depths, texts=None):\\n        import lightgbm as lgb\\n        labels = (depths > 0).astype(int)\\n        self.model = lgb.LGBMClassifier(\\n            n_estimators=200, max_depth=6, learning_rate=0.05,\\n            subsample=0.8, colsample_bytree=0.8,\\n            random_state=42, verbose=-1,\\n        )\\n        self.model.fit(features, labels)\\n\\n    def get_weights(self, features, texts=None):\\n        q = self.model.predict_proba(features)[:, 1]\\n        raw = (1.0 - q) ** self.b\\n        mean_w = raw.mean()\\n        raw = np.minimum(raw, self.rmax * mean_w)\\n        return raw / raw.max()\\n\\n    def get_mask(self, features, texts=None, threshold=None):\\n        q = self.model.predict_proba(features)[:, 1]\\n        raw = (1.0 - q) ** self.b\\n        mean_w = raw.mean()\\n        raw = np.minimum(raw, self.rmax * mean_w)\\n        prob = raw / raw.sum()\\n        rng = np.random.RandomState(self.seed)\\n        n = len(features)\\n        selected = rng.choice(n, size=int(self.k * n), replace=True, p=prob)\\n        mask = np.zeros(n, dtype=bool)\\n        mask[np.unique(selected)] = True\\n        return mask\\n\\n\\nclass Graduated(FilterStrategy):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"OBD-LightGBM: K-1 binary classifiers for P(depth>k), then exp(-alpha * E[depth]).\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n\\n    def __init__(self, alpha=0.5, n_depths=6):\\n        self.alpha = alpha\\n        self.n_depths = n_depths\\n        self.models = []\\n\\n    def fit(self, features, depths, texts=None):\\n        import lightgbm as lgb\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.7}}"}

self.ngram + 1):\\n            ng = \\\" \\\".join(tokens[i:i + self.ngram])\\n            h = int(md5(ng.encode()).hexdigest(), 16) % self.hash_dim\\n            counts[h] += 1\\n        total = sum(counts.values()) or 1\\n        return {k: v / total for k, v in counts.items()}\\n\\n    def _build_dist(self, texts):\\n        agg = np.zeros(self.hash_dim)\\n        for text in texts:\\n            for k, v in self._hash_features(text).items():\\n                agg[k] += v\\n        agg += 1e-8\\n        agg /= agg.sum()\\n        return agg\\n\\n    def fit(self, features, depths, texts=None):\\n        assert texts is not None, \\\"DSIR requires text data\\\"\\n        ref_texts = [t for t, d in zip(texts, depths) if d == 0]\\n        self.ref_dist = self._build_dist(ref_texts)\\n        self.target_dist = self._build_dist(texts)\\n\\n    def _importance_weight(self, text):\\n        feats = self._hash_features(text)\\n        log_ratio = sum(\\n            v * (np.log(self.ref_dist[k]) - np.log(self.target_dist[k]))\\n            for k, v in feats.items()\\n        )\\n        return np.exp(log_ratio)\\n\\n    def get_weights(self, features, texts=None):\\n        assert texts is not None, \\\"DSIR requires text data\\\"\\n        weights = np.array([self._importance_weight(t) for t in texts])\\n        cap = np.percentile(weights, 99)\\n        weights = np.clip(weights, 0, cap)\\n        return weights / weights.max() if weights.max() > 0 else weights\\n\\n    def get_mask(self, features, texts=None, threshold=None):\\n        weights = self.get_weights(features, texts)\\n        prob = weights / weights.sum()\\n        rng = np.random.RandomState(self.seed)\\n        n = len(features)\\n        selected = rng.choice(n, size=int(self.k * n), replace=True, p=prob)\\n        mask = np.zeros(n, dtype=bool)\\n        mask[np.unique(selected)] = True\\n        return mask\\n\\n\\nSTRATEGIES = {\\n    \\\"no-filter\\\": NoFilter,\\n    \\\"binary-ours\\\": BinaryOurs,\\n    \\\"binary-drayson\\\": BinaryDrayson,\\n    \\\"graduated\\\": Graduated,\\n    \\\"surprisal\\\": SurprisalBased,\\n    \\\"dsir\\\": DSIR,\\n}\\n\\n\\ndef load_data(data_dir):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Load features.csv and depth_*.jsonl.   doc_ids.append(obj[\\\"doc_id\\\"])\\n    return df, texts, doc_ids\\n\\n\\ndef main():\\n    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description=\\\"exp006 filtering strategies\\\")\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--data-dir\\\", type=str, default=\\\"data/\\\")\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--strategy\\\", type=str, default=\\\"graduated\\\",\\n                        choices=list(STRATEGIES.keys()))\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--alpha\\\", type=float, default=0.5,\\n                        help=\\\"Decay param for graduated strategy\\\")\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--output\\\", type=str, default=None,\\n                        help=\\\"Save weights to .npy file\\\")\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--seed\\\", type=int, default=42)\\n    args = parser.parse_args()\\n\\n    np.random.seed(args.seed)\\n\\n    print(f\\\"Loading data from {args.data_dir}\\\")\\n    df, texts, _ = load_data(args.data_dir)\\n    features = self.ngram + 1):\\n            ng = \\\" \\\".join(tokens[i:i + self.ngram])\\n            h = int(md5(ng.encode()).hexdigest(), 16) % self.hash_dim\\n            counts[h] += 1\\n        total = sum(counts.values()) or 1\\n        return {k: v / total for k, v in counts.items()}\\n\\n    def _build_dist(self, texts):\\n        agg = np.zeros(self.hash_dim)\\n        for text in texts:\\n            for k, v in self._hash_features(text).items():\\n                agg[k] += v\\n        agg += 1e-8\\n        agg /= agg.sum()\\n        return agg\\n\\n    def fit(self, features, depths, texts=None):\\n        assert texts is not None, \\\"DSIR requires text data\\\"\\n        ref_texts = [t for t, d in zip(texts, depths) if d == 0]\\n        self.ref_dist = self._build_dist(ref_texts)\\n        self.target_dist = self._build_dist(texts)\\n\\n    def _importance_weight(self, text):\\n        feats = self._hash_features(text)\\n        log_ratio = sum(\\n            v * (np.log(self.ref_dist[k]) - np.log(self.target_dist[k]))\\n            for k, v in feats.items()\\n        )\\n        return np.exp(log_ratio)\\n\\n    def get_weights(self, features, texts=None):\\n        assert texts is not None, \\\"DSIR requires text data\\\"\\n        weights = np.array([self._importance_weight(t) for t in texts])\\n        cap = np.percentile(weights, 99)\\n        weights = np.clip(weights, 0, cap)\\n        return weights / weights.max() if weights.max() > 0 else weights\\n\\n    def get_mask(self, features, texts=None, threshold=None):\\n        weights = self.get_weights(features, texts)\\n        prob = weights / weights.sum()\\n        rng = np.random.RandomState(self.seed)\\n        n = len(features)\\n        selected = rng.choice(n, size=int(self.k * n), replace=True, p=prob)\\n        mask = np.zeros(n, dtype=bool)\\n        mask[np.unique(selected)] = True\\n        return mask\\n\\n\\nSTRATEGIES = {\\n    \\\"no-filter\\\": NoFilter,\\n    \\\"binary-ours\\\": BinaryOurs,\\n    \\\"binary-drayson\\\": BinaryDrayson,\\n    \\\"graduated\\\": Graduated,\\n    \\\"surprisal\\\": SurprisalBased,\\n    \\\"dsir\\\": DSIR,\\n}\\n\\n\\ndef load_data(data_dir):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Load features.csv and depth_*.jsonl.   doc_ids.append(obj[\\\"doc_id\\\"])\\n    return df, texts, doc_ids\\n\\n\\ndef main():\\n    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description=\\\"exp006 filtering strategies\\\")\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--data-dir\\\", type=str, default=\\\"data/\\\")\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--strategy\\\", type=str, default=\\\"graduated\\\",\\n                        choices=list(STRATEGIES.keys()))\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--alpha\\\", type=float, default=0.5,\\n                        help=\\\"Decay param for graduated strategy\\\")\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--output\\\", type=str, default=None,\\n                        help=\\\"Save weights to .npy file\\\")\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--seed\\\", type=int, default=42)\\n    args = parser.parse_args()\\n\\n    np.random.seed(args.seed)\\n\\n    print(f\\\"Loading data from {args.data_dir}\\\")\\n    df, texts, _ = load_data(args.data_dir)\\n    features = strategy.fit(features, depths, texts)\\n\\n    weights = strategy.get_weights(features, texts)\\n    mask = strategy.get_mask(features, texts)\\n\\n    print(f\\\"Weights: mean={weights.mean():.4f} std={weights.std():.4f} \\\"\\n          f\\\"min={weights.min():.4f} max={weights.max():.4f}\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"Mask: {mask.sum()}/{len(mask)} kept ({mask.mean()*100:.1f}%)\\\")\\n\\n    if
```text
108	
109	We identify seven limitations that bound the generalizability of our findings.
110	
111	\emph{Model scale.}
112	All experiments use models at or below 1.5B parameters (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct).
113	Larger models may produce higher-fidelity text with weaker depth signatures, potentially shifting $\Kempirical$ beyond~2.
114	
115	\emph{Prompt-chain versus retrain-chain.}
116	Our recursive generation protocol is a prompt-chain: the same fixed model repeatedly generates from the previous output.
117	Real-world model collapse involves retrain-chains, where models are retrained on data from earlier generations~\cite{shumailov2024collapse}.
118	Retrain-chains introduce additional degradation through training dynamics, potentially producing different $\Kstar$ values.
119	Crucially, however, the prompt-chain protocol provides an \emph{upper bound} on retrain-chain $\Kstar$: our protocol holds model quality constant across generations, whereas in a retrain-chain each generation's model is trained on degraded data and is therefore weaker~\cite{shumailov2024collapse}.
120	This compounding model degradation accelerates feature convergence, implying $\Kstar^{\text{retrain}} \leq \Kstar^{\text{prompt}}$.
121	Our $\Kempirical = 2$ is thus a conservative estimate---under realistic retrain conditions, the discrimination boundary is expected to be shallower still.
122	
123	\emph{Language and domain.}
124	All experiments use English text from the C4 corpus.
125	Depth dynamics may differ across languages, domains (e.g., code, scientific text), and source corpora with different distributional properties.
126	
127	\emph{Decoding coverage.}
128	We test four decoding strategies (greedy, nucleus $p{=}0.9$, $T{=}0.7$, $T{=}1.2$).
129	Other strategies---top-$k$ sampling, beam search, contrastive decoding---remain untested and may yield different feature importance patterns or boundary behavior.
130	
131	\emph{Threshold sensitivity.}
132	$\Kempirical$ depends on the operational threshold $\theta$.
133	$\Kempirical \geq 2$ across $\theta \in \{0.55, 0.60, 0.65\}$ for all four decoding strategies, rising to $\Kempirical = 3$ at $\theta = 0.55$ (all four 2v3 $\auc$ values exceed 0.55) and returning to $\Kempirical = 2$ at $\theta \in \{0.60, 0.65\}$.
134	At $\theta = 0.70$, three of four decoding configurations collapse to $\Kempirical = 1$; only the high-temperature setting ($T{=}1.2$) retains $\Kempirical = 2$, consistent with its elevated 1v2 $\auc$ (0.7039).
135	Beyond decoding parameters, the threshold sensitivity extends to generation mode: at $\theta{=}0.70$, $\Kempirical{=}1$ for all four model-mode configurations; at $\theta{=}0.65$, continuation-mode configurations already drop to $\Kempirical{=}1$, while rewrite mode retains $\Kempirical{=}2$.
136	Our current analysis primarily covers the decoding configuration axis.
137	The boundary is robust within a practical range but not invariant to threshold choice; our conclusions are conditioned on $\theta = 0.60$.
138	
139	\emph{Boundary marginality.}
140	The $\Kempirical = 2$ determination is statistically significant but numerically marginal at both ends.
141	On the lower side, Qwen-continuation's 1v2 CI lower bound (0.6015) exceeds $\theta = 0.60$ by only $+$0.15\,pp.
142	On the upper side, Qwen-rewrite's 2v3 CI upper bound (0.5991) falls below $\theta$ by only $-$0.09\,pp.
143	This narrow margin means that small changes in the feature set or classifier could shift the boundary---as the neural probe results confirm, where a richer representation pushes the 2v3 pair above $\theta$ on the Qwen-continuation dataset (Section~\ref{sec:discuss_kstar}).
144	Importantly, all eight boundary comparisons (four 1v2 and four 2v3) yield permutation $p < 0.01$, confirming that the statistical significance of the distinction is not in doubt---only its proximity to the operational threshold.
145	
146	\emph{Operational versus theoretical boundary.}
147	$\Kempirical$ is an operational lower bound that depends on the specific feature set and classifier.
148	$\Ktheory$ may be strictly greater; a more expressive classifier or richer feature representation could push $\Kempirical$ higher.
149	Preliminary neural probing results provide direct evidence for this gap: on the Qwen-continuation dataset, a frozen-encoder MLP achieves $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}} = 3$ (2v3 $\auc = 0.6195$) compared to $\Kempirical = 2$ with 15-dimensional hand-crafted features (2v3 $\auc = 0.5609$; Section~\ref{sec:kstar_boundary}).
150	This confirms that the 15D feature set does not saturate the available depth signal and that $\Ktheory \geq 3$.
151	However, the neural probe result rests on a single dataset and awaits cross-model validation and bootstrap confidence intervals before a firm revision of the headline $\Kempirical$ can be claimed.
152	
153	\emph{Feature representation.}
154	The 15-dimensional hand-crafted feature set, while interpretable and transferable, is not optimal.
155	A preliminary neural probing experiment demonstrates that frozen LLM embeddings (1536 dimensions) paired with a lightweight MLP push $\Kempirical$ from~2 to~3 on the Qwen-continuation dataset (Section~\ref{sec:discuss_kstar}), confirming that learned representations capture depth-discriminative patterns that statistical features miss.
156	The magnitude of the gain---2v3 $\auc$ rising from 0.5609 to 0.6195---indicates meaningful recoverable signal beyond what hand-crafted features extract.
157	Full characterization of this gap, including cross-model generalization and bootstrap uncertainty quantification, remains future work.
158	
159	\paragraph{Graduated Filtering.}
160	Results for graduated filtering will be added upon completion of ongoing experiments.
161	
162	\subsection{Broader Impact}
163	\label{sec:broader_impact}
164	
165	The $\Kempirical = 2$ boundary has implications beyond the immediate scope of depth classification.
166	
167	\paragraph{Synthetic data curation.}
168	The practical consequence of $\Kempirical = 2$ is a three-tier taxonomy of text quality: human-authored (depth~0), first-generation synthetic (depth~1), and deeper synthetic (depth~$\geq 2$).
169	Texts at depth~$\geq 2$ are mutually indistinguishable and can be treated as a single category for curation purposes.
170	For practitioners filtering training data, this implies that a single round of synthetic rewriting suffices to make text statistically indistinguishable from text that has undergone multiple rounds of recycling---a form of one-wash laundering that limits the granularity of quality-based filtering.
171	This underscores the urgency of deploying depth-aware curation mechanisms before the first generation step, rather than relying on post-hoc detection.
172	Graduated filtering strategies that exploit this trichotomy may outperform binary human/synthetic filters, as the coarse ordinal signal (human / shallow / deep) captures the dominant axis of quality variation.
173	
174	\paragraph{$\Kstar$ as a corpus audit metric.}
175	Independent of filtering applications, $\Kstar$ provides an actionable corpus quality metric answering a concrete question: what fraction of a training corpus consists of depth-$\geq 2$ synthetic text?
176	Since $\Kempirical = 2$ implies that texts at depth~$\geq 2$ are statistically indistinguishable from depth-1 text, they should be treated equivalently in data curation.
177	This establishes a directly actionable threshold: texts exceeding $\Kstar$ generations require no finer-grained depth discrimination and can be assigned to a single ``deep synthetic'' category, simplifying dataset audits even when the efficacy of graduated filtering remains to be established.
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	% === Contribution 1: K*=2 consistent boundary ===
5	% Sources: exp_007 (bootstrap CI + permutation), exp_001/exp_002/exp_017/mvp_pilot (model-mode), exp_003 (decoding ablation)
6	We presented the first systematic measurement of the generational depth
7	discrimination boundary $\Kempirical$ for recursive synthetic text.
8	Across three model architectures in four model-mode configurations
9	(Pythia-1.4B and OLMo-1B in continuation mode; Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct in both
10	continuation and rewrite modes) and 4 decoding strategies---totaling 8
11	conditions---we establish $\Kempirical = 2$ as a consistent boundary:
12	classifiers reliably distinguish adjacent generations up to depth~2, but all
13	deeper pairs fall below the operational threshold $\theta = 0.60$.
14	For the four model-mode configurations, bootstrap 95\% confidence intervals
15	and permutation tests confirm statistical significance at the 1v2 boundary
16	($p \leq 0.002$ for all four); the most marginal case, Qwen-continuation,
17	yields a CI lower bound of 0.6015, exceeding $\theta$ by only 0.15\,pp.
18	All four decoding configurations (greedy, nucleus, $T{=}0.70$, $T{=}1.2$)
19	likewise yield 1v2 point-estimate $\auc$ above $\theta$.
20	The 0v1 boundary is near-perfectly separable ($\auc > 0.99$), followed by a
21	sharp ${\sim}$30\,pp drop at 1v2 ($\auc$: 0.6112--0.7039 across all
22	conditions), while all 2v3 pairs fall below~$\theta$.
23	Recursive generation thus acts as a severely lossy channel whose discriminable
24	statistical fingerprint is exhausted within two steps.
25	
26	% === Contribution 2: Ordinal depth estimation framework ===
27	% Sources: exp_010 (OBD vs baselines), exp_013 (cross-model transfer), exp_005 (feature degradation), exp_011 (LLM zero-shot)
28	To operationalize depth estimation, we introduced an ordinal binary
29	decomposition (OBD) framework pairing 15 hand-crafted statistical features
30	with LightGBM classifiers.
31	Cross-model transfer experiments demonstrate 80--92\% accuracy retention
32	across continuation-mode model pairs, with
33	Pythia$\leftrightarrow$OLMo achieving 90--92\% and 0v1 transfer $\auc$
34	exceeding 0.96 across all tested model pairs---indicating that depth signals are largely
35	text-intrinsic rather than generator-specific.
36	Feature degradation orderings are consistent across architectures
37	(Spearman $\rho \geq 0.85$), with surprisal-variance features dominating
38	across all tested families, suggesting that the two-step information loss is driven primarily by the
39	autoregressive mechanism at this scale.
40	As a reference point, frontier LLMs (GPT-5.5, Claude Opus~4.6) achieve
41	${\sim}$88\% binary human-vs.-synthetic accuracy but only ${\sim}$33\% on
42	5-class depth estimation, below OBD-LightGBM's 38.6\%---confirming that
43	fine-grained depth discrimination requires specialized statistical features
44	rather than general language understanding.
45	A preliminary neural probing experiment suggests that this operational bound is not tight: a frozen Qwen2.5-1.5B encoder paired with an MLP classifier achieves $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}} = 3$ on the Qwen-continuation dataset (2v3 $\auc = 0.6195$ versus 0.5609 with hand-crafted features), indicating that learned representations recover depth signal beyond the 15-dimensional feature set.
46	This result, pending cross-model validation, provides initial evidence that $\Ktheory \geq 3$.
47	
48	% === Contribution 3: Graduated filtering (C3) ===
49	% Source: exp_006 (RUNNING — results pending)
50	The $\Kempirical = 2$ boundary implies a natural trichotomy---human (depth~0), first-generation synthetic (depth~1), and deeper synthetic (depth~$\geq 2$)---that can be exploited for depth-aware data curation.
51	% TODO: exp-006 graduated filtering 具体结果填充。
52	% 预留内容：graduated filtering 利用三级分类法在 MMLU/HellaSwag 上的表现，
53	% 与 binary filtering 和 no-filter baseline 的对比。
54	
55	% === Limitations + Future Work ===
56	Our findings are bounded by the scale of models tested ($\leq$1.5B parameters), restriction to English C4 text, and the use of prompt-chain rather than retrain-chain protocols; $\Kempirical$ depends on the threshold $\theta$ and shifts with generation mode (continuation-mode configurations drop to $\Kempirical{=}1$ at $\theta{=}0.65$, while rewrite mode retains $\Kempirical{=}2$; Section~\ref{sec:limitations}); the 15-dimensional feature set yields $\Kempirical$ as an operational lower bound, and $\Ktheory$ may be strictly higher.
57	Several directions merit investigation: scaling to larger models (7B+) to test whether increased generation fidelity shifts the boundary; extending to multilingual and multi-domain corpora; establishing the relationship between prompt-chain and retrain-chain $\Kstar$ values, which would connect our measurements to the model collapse setting; incorporating few-shot or chain-of-thought prompting for LLM-based depth estimation; and extending the neural probe approach---which has yielded preliminary evidence of $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}} = 3$ on one dataset---through cross-model validation (Pythia, OLMo), bootstrap confidence intervals, multi-layer probing beyond the last hidden state, and comparison with fine-tuned (non-frozen) encoders to further narrow the gap between $\Kempirical$ and $\Ktheory$.
58	
```

_source line 605 · 2026-05-14T08:45:26.722Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01GMNvazuNYLYNPmMt54quhp`</summary>

```text
177	This establishes a directly actionable threshold: texts exceeding $\Kstar$ generations require no finer-grained depth discrimination and can be assigned to a single ``deep synthetic'' category, simplifying dataset audits even when the efficacy of graduated filtering remains to be established.
178	
179	\paragraph{Relation to model collapse.}
180	Shumailov et al.~\cite{shumailov2024collapse} and Alemohammad et al.~\cite{alemohammad2024selfconsuming} study model collapse under \emph{retrain-chains}, where models are iteratively trained on their own outputs.
181	Our prompt-chain protocol measures an adjacent but distinct phenomenon: the degradation of statistical distinguishability under recursive generation by a fixed model.
182	The rapid convergence we observe ($\Kempirical = 2$) is consistent with the theoretical prediction of convergence to a fixed point, but the precise relationship between prompt-chain $\Kempirical$ and retrain-chain convergence rates remains an open question.
183	If prompt-chain depth signals serve as an observable proxy for early-stage model collapse, depth estimation could provide a lightweight diagnostic for data curation pipelines without requiring expensive retraining experiments.
184	
185	\paragraph{AI text detection.}
186	Current AI text detection is predominantly binary: human versus machine.
187	Our results suggest that \emph{depth-aware} detection provides a strictly more informative signal.
188	The near-perfect 0v1 separability ($\auc > 0.99$) confirms that binary detection is reliable at this scale, but the 1v2 boundary ($\auc$: 0.61--0.70) reveals that first-generation text retains measurable statistical distance from deeper synthetic text.
189	This additional dimension---how many generations of processing a text has undergone---may prove valuable for provenance tracking and corpus quality assessment, where the binary human/AI label elides important distinctions between lightly and heavily recycled content.
190	
```

</details>

```text
35:These results are preliminary: they cover a single model-dataset pair (Qwen-continuation) and lack bootstrap confidence intervals; cross-model validation on Pythia and OLMo is pending.
76:This result constitutes a \emph{methodological lower bound}: the framework itself---15-dimensional token-level features, OBD-optimized classification, bootstrap confidence intervals, and permutation testing---is model-scale agnostic by construction.
93:On a 500-sample test set with 5 depth classes (depths 0--4), excluding depth~5 from the 6-class range used in the main feature-based experiments since depths $\geq 3$ are mutually indistinguishable ($\Kempirical = 2$), GPT-5.5 achieves 33.2\% accuracy (MAE = 1.10) and Claude Opus~4.6 achieves 33.1\% accuracy (MAE = 1.15)---both below the OBD-LightGBM baseline of 38.6\% (MAE = 1.11; see Table~\ref{tab:s3_llm_zeroshot} for per-depth confusion matrices).
102:[Omitted long matching line]
151:However, the neural probe result rests on a single dataset and awaits cross-model validation and bootstrap confidence intervals before a firm revision of the headline $\Kempirical$ can be claimed.
183:If prompt-chain depth signals serve as an observable proxy for early-stage model collapse, depth estimation could provide a lightweight diagnostic for data curation pipelines without requiring expensive retraining experiments.
```

```text
2	  "raw": "Inserted at `discussion.tex:101-103` (Section 5.5, between the existing qualitative argument and the future-work caveat). Full paragraph (~80 words):\n\n> Per-depth analysis sharpens this conclusion. Both models achieve high depth-0 recall (GPT-5.5: 0.77, Claude: 0.69), indicating reliable human-text identification, but recall for depths 1–4 collapses to 0.08–0.38, with confusion matrices (Table S3) showing predictions scattered nearly uniformly across non-human classes. The resulting Spearman correlations between predicted and true depth are weak (ρ = 0.38 for GPT-5.5, ρ = 0.42 for Claude), confirming that frontier LLMs function as binary human-vs.-AI detectors augmented by near-random depth guessing—confidence scores cannot serve as a depth proxy.\n\nData sources: Spearman ρ values from the exp-011 checkpoint; per-depth recall and confusion matrices from Table S3 in appendix.tex."
3	}
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Jxp4xq91a5PCJkeR6T3jqx`</summary>

```text
96	This gap between binary detection and depth estimation is informative.
97	Frontier LLMs can reliably determine \emph{whether} a text is machine-generated but cannot estimate \emph{how many generations of recursive processing} it has undergone.
98	Depth estimation requires sensitivity to subtle distributional shifts between adjacent generations---shifts orders of magnitude smaller than the human-vs.-machine gap.
99	The 15-dimensional statistical feature pipeline, despite its simplicity, captures these fine-grained shifts more effectively than frontier LLMs in a zero-shot setting.
100	This motivates the dedicated feature-engineering approach: the task demands specialized statistical sensitivity rather than general language understanding.
101	
102	Per-depth analysis sharpens this conclusion. Both models achieve high depth-0 recall (GPT-5.5: 0.77, Claude: 0.69), indicating reliable human-text identification, but recall for depths~1--4 collapses to 0.08--0.38, with confusion matrices (Table~\ref{tab:s3_llm_zeroshot}) showing predictions scattered nearly uniformly across non-human classes. The resulting Spearman correlations between predicted and true depth are weak ($\rho = 0.38$ for GPT-5.5, $\rho = 0.42$ for Claude), confirming that frontier LLMs function as binary human-vs.-AI detectors augmented by near-random depth guessing---confidence scores cannot serve as a depth proxy.
103	
104	This conclusion extends to statistical detection tools.
105	Methods such as DetectGPT~\cite{mitchell2023detectgpt} and Binoculars~\cite{hans2024binoculars} optimize a scalar score for the binary human-vs.-AI boundary---the same distinction where our LLM baselines excel (${\sim}88\%$ accuracy).
106	But the per-depth recall collapse (0.08--0.38 for depths 1--4) demonstrates that binary detection confidence is orthogonal to generational depth: a high ``AI-generated'' score carries no information about how many generation steps a text has undergone.
107	Depth estimation is a fundamentally distinct task requiring multi-dimensional statistical sensitivity that scalar detection scores do not provide.
```
